Implicit Regularization for Tubal Tensor Factorizations via Gradient Descent
Santhosh Karnik *, Anna Veselovska *, Mark Iwen, Felix Krahmer

Abstract
We provide a rigorous analysis of implicit regularization in an overparametrized tensor factorization problem beyond the lazy training regime. For matrix factorization problems, this phenomenon has been studied in a number of works. A particular challenge has been to design universal initialization strategies which provably lead to implicit regularization in gradient-descent methods. At the same time, it has been argued by [ 7 ] that more general classes of neural networks can be captured by considering tensor factorizations. However, in the tensor case, implicit regularization has only been rigorously established for gradient flow or in the lazy training regime. In this paper, we prove the first tensor result of its kind for gradient descent rather than gradient flow. We focus on the tubal tensor product and the associated notion of low tubal rank, encouraged by the relevance of this model for image data. We establish that gradient descent in an overparametrized tensor factorization model with a small random initialization exhibits an implicit bias towards solutions of low tubal rank. Our theoretical findings are illustrated in an extensive set of numerical simulations show-casing the dynamics predicted by our theory as well as the crucial role of using a small random initialization.

1
Introduction

Analyzing implicit regularization during Neural Network (NN) training is considered crucial for understanding why overparametrization can give rise to superior generalization capability and lead to strong overall NN performance. Consequently, there has been a recent surge in research aimed at explaining how gradient-based methods interact with overparameterized models under nonconvex losses (see, e.g.,
[
28
,
24
]
). Notably, recent empirical and theoretical studies have suggested that gradient-based methods with small random initializations exhibit a bias towards low-rank solutions in a variety of models.

For matrix factorization models which represent linear neural networks, a rigorous analysis of implicit bias is available for both gradient descent
[
12
,
36
]
and gradient flow (its asymptotic limit for small step size)
[
3
,
6
]
.
In contrast, for neural networks with nonlinear activation, there has been a good deal of work done showing that fully connected layers can be represented by, e.g., tensor train factorizations in
[
29
,
32
]
. As a consequence, it has been argued that tensor factorizations should be considered instead of matrix factorizations (see, e.g.,
[
7
]
). For tensor factorization models, however, results predating 2024 were only available for the asymptotic regime, i.e., gradient flow. This is perhaps due to the many additional complications in the tensor setting beyond those in the matrix setting including, e.g, that there are many different valid notions of tensor rank, each of which motivates its own equally valid class of tensor factorizations.
For gradient descent applied to the tensor recovery problem, only a very recent partial analysis by
[
27
]
currently exists for the tubal factorization model. This analysis requires that the initialization already well approximates the solution, only after which the convergence of gradient descent toward a low tubal-rank solution is shown. Herein we also focus on the tubal factorization, but establish the corresponding implicit regularization result without needing such a strong initialization assumption.

Related work:

In deep learning it is common to use more network parameters than training points. In such overparameterized scenarios there are usually many networks that achieve zero training error so that the training algorithm effectively imposes an implicit regularization (bias) on the solution it computes. In practice, training networks with gradient descent is both common and tends to favor solutions that generalize well, offering the exploration of how gradient descent implicitly regularlizes in overparameterized regimes as one avenue for better understanding the success of deep learning more widely. As a result, a lot of recent work has been focussed on understanding the implicit regularization phenomena of gradient descent in multiple settings. The first theoretical works in this direction
[
13
,
12
,
9
,
2
,
35
]

concentrated on training linear networks and suggested that during training (stochastic) gradient descent implicitly converges to a linear network (i.e., a linear function described by a matrix) that’s low rank.
Motivated by specific deep learning tasks, multiple works also investigated implicit bias phenomena in the special cases of sparse vector and low-rank matrix recovery from underdetermined measurements via
an overparameterized square loss functional, where the vectors and matrices to be reconstructed
were deeply factorized into several vector/matrix factors. In this setting, these works then showed that the dynamics of vanilla gradient descent are biased towards sparse/low-rank solutions, respectively
[
6
,
5
,
23
,
20
]
.

In the realm of optimization, a substantial body of work has also emerged that provides guarantees for gradient descent’s convergence in the nonconvex setting for different problems such as phase retrieval, matrix completion,
and blind deconvolution. Broadly, these findings can be categorized into two main approaches: smart initialization coupled with local convergence (demonstrating, e.g., local convergence of descent techniques starting from carefully designed spectral initializations)
[
28
,
38
,
24
,
4
]
; and landscape analysis paired with saddle-escaping algorithms which show, e.g., that all local minima are global and that saddle points exhibit strict negative curvature so that (stochastic) gradient-based methods can effectively escape saddles and ensure convergence to global minimizers
[
16
,
8
,
30
]
.

Notably, several studies
[
43
,
10
]
have highlighted the importance of the scale of the training initialization for the generalization and test performance of modern machine learning architectures. In fact, a small random initialization followed by (stochastic) gradient descent is arguably the most widely used training algorithm in contemporary machine learning. And, stronger generalization performance is typically observed with smaller-scale initializations.
Implicit bias for low-rank matrix recovery with small random initializations has been extensively studied in this setting as a result by, e.g.,
[
36
,
34
,
42
,
19
]
. These studies have shown that a small random Gaussian initialization behaves similarly to a spectral initialization in overparameterized settings. Furthermore, they have shown that gradient descent algorithms with this initialization tend to converge towards low-rank solutions (i.e., that they demonstrate an implicit regularization towards low-rank solutions).

Recently, numerous connections between tensor decompositions and training neural networks have also been established by, e.g.,
[
29
,
32
,
31
]
. These studies argue that low-rank tensor factorization helps explain implicit regularization in deep learning, as well as how properties of real-world data translate this regularization to generalization. Similar to how matrix factorization can be viewed as a linear neural network (i.e., a fully connected network with linear activation), tensor factorizations correspond to a specific type of shallow (depth-two) nonlinear convolutional neural network
[
7
,
32
]
. Additionally,
[
29
]
demonstrated that the dense weight matrices of fully connected layers can be converted to tensor trains while preserving the layer’s expressive power. These findings have positioned low-rank tensor factorizations as theoretical surrogates for various neural network learning settings, thereby enhancing our understanding of implicit regularization and overparameterization, and so further motivating investigation in this area.

Since no unique definition of tensor rank is available, related literature concerning implicit bias has naturally split with respect to the notion of tensor rank being considered: CP-rank, Tucker-rank, and tubal-rank, in analogy to the analysis of algorithms specifically designed for tensor recovery and completion by, e.g.,
[
44
,
15
,
21
,
1
,
26
,
25
,
14
]
. For the CP-tensor factorization, several results are available for gradient-based methods. The first theoretical analysis of
implicit regularization towards low tensor rank under arbitrarily small initialization was provided considering gradient flow in
[
32
]
. In
[
8
]
, it has been shown for the orthogonal tensor decomposition problem a simple variant of the stochastic gradient algorithm is able to leverage a low-rank structure from an arbitrary starting point. In addition,
[
40
]
shows that using gradient descent on an over-parametrized objective for the CP-rank tensor
decomposition problem one could go beyond the lazy training regime and utilize certain low-rank structures.

Perhaps most closely related to this paper, very recently
[
27
]
analyzed the convergence of factorized gradient descent for the low-tubal-rank sensing problem, showing that with carefully designed spectral initialization the gradient iterates converge to a low-tubal rank tensor. Although the authors in
[
27
]
allow for overparametrization, they argue the minimal recovery error can be achieved when knowing the true rank, thereby leaving questions concerning the advantages of overparametrization and small random initializations open.

Figure 1
:
A low tubal-rank factorization of a three-dimensional tensor. Using the (reduced) tubal-SVD, each three-dimensional tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m\times k}
can be decomposed into a tubal product of three tensors
\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\bm{\mathcal{V}}*\bm{\Sigma}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}^{\top}
with
\bm{\mathcal{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
,
\bm{\mathcal{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m\times k}
and the frontal slice diagonal tensor
\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m\times k}
. Here, the tubal rank of a tensor is the number of non-zero singular tubes in
\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m\times k}
. For example, in the figure, the tubal rank of the tensor is equal to six.

Our contribution:

Motivated by connections between tensor rank and non-linear neural network representations, herein we study the implicit regularization phenomenon for low tubal-rank tensor recovery. Namely, our objective is to analyze the recovery process of a tensor with a low tubal-rank factorization
[
17
]
(see Fig 1) from a limited number of random linear measurements. More specifically, we consider tensors of the form
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
and employ a non-convex method based on the tensor factorization, minimizing the loss function using gradient descent with a small random initialization. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to investigate the implicit bias phenomenon for gradient descent with a small random initialization applied to a tensor factorization.
Namely, we demonstrate that, irrespective of the degree of overparameterization, vanilla gradient descent with a small random initialization applied to a tubal tensor factorization will consistently converge to a low tubal-rank solution.

Inspired by recent results for the low-rank matrix sensing problem by
[
36
]
, we establish that gradient descent iterates with small random initializations can be closely approximated by power method iterations in
[
11
,
18
]
modulo normalization, and deduce that after sufficient time the iterates approach a commonly used spectral initialization from the tubal-rank literature in
[
27
]
. Along the way we must also overcome, e.g., a challenging intersection between the tensor slices during each gradient descent iterate which forces a non-trivial convergence analysis.

Organization:

In Section
2
, we define our notation and present a few basic facts regarding tubal tensors. In Section
3
, we state our problem and our main result. In Section
4
, we outline the steps of the proof in order to provide intuition. In Section
5
, we show numerical experiments which demonstrate our theoretical findings. We conclude the paper in Section
6
. The proof of our main result is broken up into several lemmas, which are stated and proven in the appendix.

2
Notation and Preliminaries

Every tensor in this paper will be an order-
3
tensor whose third mode is length
k
. For such a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
, we define a block-diagonal Fourier domain representation by

\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\text{blockdiag}(\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{(1)},\ldots,\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{(k)})\in\mathbb{C}^{mk\times nk}

where the
j
-th block
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
is defined by
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{(j)}(i,i^{\prime})=\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k}\bm{\mathcal{T}}(i,i^{\prime},j^{\prime})e^{-\sqrt{-1}2\pi(j-1)(j^{\prime}-1)/k}.
In other words, we take the FFT of each tube, and then arrange the resulting frontal slices into a block-diagonal matrix.

The tubal product (or t-product) of two tubal tensors
\bm{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times q\times k}
and
\bm{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times n\times k}
is a tubal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}*\bm{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
whose tubes are given by

(\bm{\mathcal{A}}*\bm{\mathcal{B}})(i,i^{\prime},:)=\sum_{p=1}^{q}\bm{\mathcal{A}}(i,p,:)*\bm{\mathcal{B}}(p,i^{\prime},:)

where
*
denotes the circular convolution of the two tubes. One can check that
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{A}}*\bm{\mathcal{B}}}=\overline{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}\ \overline{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}
.

For any tubal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
, its tubal transpose
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m\times k}
is given by
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\top})(i,i^{\prime},1)=\bm{\mathcal{T}}(i^{\prime},i,1)
and
(\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\top})(i,i^{\prime},j)=\bm{\mathcal{T}}(i^{\prime},i,k+2-j)
for
j=2,\ldots,k
, i.e., we take the transpose of each face, and then reverse the order of frontal slices
j=2,\ldots,k
. This ensures that
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\top}}=\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\top}
.

For any
n
, the
n\times n\times k
identity tensor
\bm{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
is defined by
\bm{\mathcal{I}}(:,:,1)=I_{n\times n}
(identity matrix), and
\bm{\mathcal{I}}(:,:,j)=0_{n\times n}
(zero matrix). An orthogonal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
satisfies
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}*\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}=\bm{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{Q}}=\bm{\mathcal{I}}
. An orthonormal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
with
m\geq n
satisfies
\bm{\mathcal{W}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}=\bm{\mathcal{I}}
.

The tubal-SVD
[
17
]
(or t-SVD) of a tubal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
is a factorization of the form

\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}*\bm{\Sigma}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}^{\top}

(2.1)

where
\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m\times k}
and
\bm{\mathcal{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
are orthogonal, and each frontal slice of
\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
is diagonal. The t-SVD of a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
can be computed as follows: (1) compute the FFT of each tube of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
to get the frontal slices
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{(j)}
,
j=1,\ldots,k
, (2) compute the SVD of each resulting frontal slice
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{(j)}=\overline{U}^{(j)}\overline{\Sigma}^{(j)}\overline{V}^{(j)\top}
, (3) concatenate the matrices
\{\overline{U}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{k}
into a tubal tensor
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m\times k}
and take the inverse FFT along mode-
3
to obtain
\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m\times k}
(and similarly to obtain
\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
and
\bm{\mathcal{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
). Finally, the tubal rank of a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
is the number of non-zero diagonal tubes in the
\bm{\Sigma}
tensor of its t-SVD, i.e.,
\operatorname{rank}(\bm{\mathcal{T}})=\#\{i:\bm{\Sigma}(i,i,:)\neq 0\}
. For an illustration of the t-SVD decomposition, see Figure
1
.
We also define the condition number
\kappa(\bm{\mathcal{T}})
of the tubal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n\times k}
by

\kappa(\bm{\mathcal{T}}):=\dfrac{\sigma_{1}(\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})}{\sigma_{\min\{m,n\}k}(\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})}.

3
Main Results

Problem Formulation

Let
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
have tubal rank
r\leq n
so that
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\in S^{n\times n\times k}_{+}
is a tubal positive definite tensor with tubal rank
r
. Let
\kappa=\kappa(\bm{\mathcal{X}})
be the condition number of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
. Suppose we observe
m
linear measurements of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
, that is

y_{i}=\left<\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i},\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\right>\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,m

(3.1)

where each
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\in S^{n\times n\times k}
is a tubal-symmetric tensor. We can write this compactly as
\bm{y}=\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})
where
\mathcal{A}:S^{n\times n\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the linear measurement operator.
We aim to recover
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
from our measurements
\bm{y}
by using gradient descent to learn an overparameterized factorization. Specifically, we fix an
R\geq r
and try to find a
\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}
such that
\bm{\mathcal{U}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\top}=\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
by using gradient descent to minimize the loss function ´

\displaystyle\ell(\bm{\mathcal{U}}):

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{A}\left(\bm{\mathcal{U}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\top}\right)-\bm{y}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(3.2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\left<\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i},\bm{\mathcal{U}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\top}\right>-y_{i}\right)^{2}.

(3.3)

We will start with a small random initialization
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}
where each entry is i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\tfrac{\alpha^{2}}{R})
for some small
\alpha>0
. Then, the gradient descent iterations are given by

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\mu\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}+\mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\left[\bm{y}-\mathcal{A}\left(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}\right)\right]*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu(\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A})\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}\right)\right]*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}

(3.4)

for some suitably small stepsize
\mu>0
. Here
\mathcal{A}^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to S^{n\times n\times k}
denotes the adjoint of
\mathcal{A}
which is given by
\mathcal{A}^{*}\bm{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bm{z}_{i}\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.

Moreover, we say that a measurement operator
\mathcal{A}:S^{n\times n\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies the Restricted Isometry Property (RIP) of rank-
r
with constant
\delta>0
(abbreviated
\text{RIP}(r,\delta)
), if we have

(1-\delta)\|\bm{\mathcal{Z}}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{Z}})\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1+\delta)\|\bm{\mathcal{Z}}\|_{F}^{2},

for all
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}\in S^{n\times n\times k}
with tubal-rank
\leq r
.

Results

We have analyzed the convergence process of the gradient descent iterates (
3.4
) in the scenario of small random initialization and overparametrization. Namely, with the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
, we assume the initialization
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}
is such that each entry is i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\tfrac{\alpha^{2}}{r})
with small scaling parameter
\alpha>0
and the second dimension
R
exceeding the ground truth dimension
r
. Below, we present the direct
results of our analysis.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose we have
m
linear measurements
y=\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})
of a tubal positive semidefinite tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\in S^{n\times n\times k}_{+}
where
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
has tubal rank
r\leq n
. We assume
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
\text{RIP}(2r+1,\delta)
with
\delta\leq c\kappa^{-4}r^{-1/2}
. Suppose we fit a model
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}^{\top}
where
\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}
with
R\geq r
and obtain
\bm{\mathcal{U}}
by running the gradient descent iterations

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t+1}=\left[\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu(\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A})\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}\right)\right]*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}

starting from the initialization
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}
where each entry is i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\tfrac{\alpha^{2}}{r})
. Then, if the overparametrized model satisfies
R\geq 3r
and the scale of the initialization satisfies

\alpha\lesssim\dfrac{\sigma_{\text{min}}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})}{\kappa^{2}\min\{n,R\}\sqrt{k}}\left(\dfrac{C_{2}\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{\min\{n,R\}}}\right)^{-16\kappa^{2}},

then after

\widehat{t}\lesssim\dfrac{1}{\mu\sigma_{\text{min}}(\bm{\mathcal{X}})^{2}}\ln\left(\dfrac{C_{1}\kappa n}{\min\{n,R\}}\min\left\{1,\dfrac{\kappa r}{k(\min\{n,R\}-r)}\right\}\dfrac{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|}{k\alpha}\right)

iterations, we have that

\dfrac{\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{\widehat{t}}\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{\widehat{t}}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|^{2}}\lesssim k^{\tfrac{61}{32}}r^{\tfrac{1}{8}}\kappa^{-\tfrac{3}{16}}(\min\{n,R\}-r)^{\tfrac{3}{8}}\left(\dfrac{C_{2}\kappa^{2}\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{\min\{n,R\}}}\right)^{21\kappa^{2}}\left(\dfrac{\alpha}{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}\|}\right)^{\tfrac{21}{16}}

holds with probability at least
1-Cke^{-\tilde{c}r}
. Here,
c,\tilde{c},C,C_{1},C_{2}>0
are fixed numerical constants. Here,
c,C_{1},C_{2}>0
are fixed numerical constants.

Intuitively, this means that if the initialization is sufficiently small, gradient descent will recover the low tubal rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
. Note that the reconstruction error can be made arbitrarily small by making the size of the random initialization
\alpha
arbitrarily small. This comes at the expense of requiring more iterations. However, this impact is mild as the number of iterations grows only logarithmically with respect to
\alpha
.

Figure 2
:
Illustration of (a) the two stages of gradient descent algorithm: the spectral alignment stage for
1\leq t\lesssim 3000
and the convergence stage
3000\lesssim t
and (b) more details on the alignment phase for the gradient descent progress. In the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
, we set
n=10,k=4,r=3
.

4
Proof Outline

In this section, we turn our attention to giving an overview of the key ideas of the proof.

In our analysis, we demonstrate that the trajectory of gradient descent iterations can be approximately divided into two distinct stages: (I) a spectral stage and (II) a convergence stage described below.

(I) The spectral stage.
In the spectral stage, where we show that the gradient descent starting from random initialization behaves similarly to spectral initialization, enabling us to prove that by the end of this stage, the column spaces of the tensor iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
(
3.4
) and the ground truth matrix
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
are sufficiently aligned. Namely, we show that the first few iterations of the gradient descent algorithm
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
can be approximated by the iteration of the tensor power method modulo normalization (see, e.g.
[
11
]
) defined as

\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}=\Big(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\Big)^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}.

We call this part of the evolution of the gradient descent iteration the “spectral stage” since, due to its similarity to the power method, at the end of this stage the iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
will be closely aligned with the classical t-SVD spectral initialization of
[
27
]
.

(II) The convergence stage
. In the convergence stage, the gradient iterates converge approximately to the underlying low tubal-rank tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
at a geometric rate until reaching a certain error floor which is dependent on the initialization scale.

The cornerstone of the analysis of this stage is the decomposition of the tensor gradient iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
into two components, the so-called “signal” and “noise” terms. This is done by adapting similar decomposition methods used in recent works analyzing implicit bias phenomenon for gradient descent in the matrix setting (see
[
36
,
22
]
) to our tensor setting.
Accordingly, let the tensor-column subspace of the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
be denoted by
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
with the corresponding basis
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
. Consider the tensor
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times R\times k}
with its t-SVD decomposition
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{t}*\bm{\Sigma}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}
. For
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times r\times k}
, we denote by
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(n-r)\times k}
a tensor whose tensor-column subspace is orthogonal to those of
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}
, that is
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}\|=0
and its projection operator
\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}}
is defined as
{\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}}=\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}=\bm{\mathcal{I}}-\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}}
.

We then decompose the gradient descent iterates (
3.4
) as follows

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}+\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}

(4.1)

referring to the tensors
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}
as the signal term of the gradient descent iterates, and to the tensors
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}
as the noise term. The advantage of such a decomposition is that the tensor-column space of the noise term
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}
is orthogonal to the tensor-column subspace of the ground truth
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
allowing for a rigorous analysis of the convergence process of the two components separately.

At the convergence stage, we show that symmetric tensor
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}
built from the signal term
converges towards the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
,
whereas the spectral norm of the noise term
\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}\|
, stays small.

(b)

(c)

Outcomes of employing gradient descent to minimize the loss function (
3.2
) with different overparametrization rates. We set
n=10,k=4,r=3
in the ground truth tensor

and for initialization

R=10,50,100,200,400
. For each
R
we plot the average over twenty experiments. The plots (a),(b), and (d) are semi-log plots.

Additional challenges in the tensor setting vs. matrix setting

When coming from the matrix case to the tensor setting com, there are several important differences and challenges, which need to be carefully considered and are described below.

•

In contrast to the matrix case, the range and kernel of a third-order tubal tensor can include overlapping generator elements (we refrain from using the term basis, in the
sense that knowledge of the multirank and complimentary tubal scalar of a tensor must be included to describe the range). Namely, if in the t-SVD (
2.1
) of a symmetric tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
the tensor
\bm{\Sigma}
contains
q
non-invertible tubes – tubes that have zero elements in the Fourier domain –, then there are
q
common generators for the range and the kernel of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
, please see
[
18
]
for more details. With this phenomenon, the decomposition (
C.1
) of the gradient iterates into signal and noise term is not available for non-invertible tubes, which is why we need to work with a more intricate notion of condition number.

•

As stated in
[
11
]
, running the power method for tubal tensors of dimensions
n\times n\times k
is equivalent to running in parallel
k
independent matrix power methods in Fourier domain. However, running gradient descent in the tubal tensor setting is not equivalent to running
k
gradient descent algorithms independently in Fourier space. This can be easily seen when transforming the measurement operator part of the gradient descent iterates. Namely, let as before
y=\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
y_{i}=\left<\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i},\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\right>=\left<\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\right>=\sum_{q=1}^{k}\left<\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(q)},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(q)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(q)\mathrm{H}}\right>,\,j=1,\ldots m

then
\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})=\mathcal{A}^{*}(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\in S^{n\times n\times k}
and the for
j
-th slice in the Fourier domain, we get
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\left<\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(q)},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(q)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(q)\mathrm{H}}\right>.
This means that in each Fourier slice
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
of the gradient descent iterates (
3.4
) we have the full information about the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
and not only about its
j
-th slice. In the spectral stage, this fact does not cause significant difficulties. However, in the convergence stage, in order to get the global estimates, it requires a thorough and vigilant analysis of intersections between the slices in the Fourier domain.

5
Numerical Experiments

To verify our theoretical
findings, we set multiple numerical tests: from showing two phases of the gradient descent algorithm to demonstrating the advantages of overparametrization. These experimental
results showcase not only the implicit regularization for the gradient descent algorithm toward low-tubal-rank tensors but also demonstrate the firmness of our theoretical findings.

Our experiments were conducted on a MacBook Pro equipped with an Apple M1 processor and 16GB of memory, using MATLAB 2023a
software.

We generate the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
with tubal rank
r
by
\bm{\mathcal{T}}=\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
, where the entries of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
are i.i.d. sampled from a Gaussian distribution

\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and then
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
is normalized. The entries of measurement tensor
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
are i.i.d.
sampled from a Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{m})
. In the following, we describe different testing scenarios for recovery of
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
via the gradient descent algorithm and their outcome. For all the experiments, we set the dimensions to
n=10,k=4,r=3
, the learning rate
\mu=10^{-5}
, and the number of measurements
m=254
.

Illustration of the two convergence stages.

To illustrate the convergence process of the gradient iterates, for the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
and its counterpart
{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}*{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
being learned by the gradient descent, we consider the training error
\ell(U_{t})
, the test error
\frac{\|{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}*{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}}
, and the test error for their
r
th singular tubes
\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}),\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\frac{\|\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t})-\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\|_{2}}{\|\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\|_{2}}
.
Moreover, we also take into our consideration the tensor subspace
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
spanned
by the tensor-columns corresponding to the first
r
singular-tubes of the tensor
\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})
and
denote by
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
the tensor-column subspace spanned by the tensor-columns corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}
. Figures
2(a)
and
2(b)
demonstrate that the convergence analysis can be divided into
two stages: the spectral and the convergence
stage. We see that in the first stage (
1\leq t\lesssim 3000
), the first
r
tensor-columns of
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}
learn the tensor column subspace
corresponding to the first
r
singular-tubes of the tensor
\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})
, i.e. the principal angle between the tensor column subspaces
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
becomes small. Namely, as one can observe in Figure
2(b)
, the principal angle between the two subspaces,
\|\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}}\|
, decreases where as the principal angle between
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
reaches certain plateau, see the behavior of
\|\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\perp}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}}\|
. At the same time, test errors
\frac{\|{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}*{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}}
and
\frac{\|\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t})-\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\|_{2}}{\|\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\|_{2}}
stay large. In the second stage, we see that the test error
\frac{\|{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}*{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\|_{F}}
starts decreasing, meaning that the gradient descent iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}
start converging to
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
by learning more about the tensor-column subspace of the ground truth tensor. At the same time, the test error over
r
th singular tube
\frac{\|\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t})-\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\|_{2}}{\|\sigma_{r}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}})\|_{2}}
starts decreasing too and as a result converges to zero. We also see that in this stage
the principal angle between
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
grows, which is also intuitive as the tensor-column subspace
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
does not have the full information about the tensor-column subspace of the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
, and learning more about
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
leads to a larger error in terms of principal angles of the two.

Depiction of the alignment stage.

In this experiment, we illustrate that gradient
descent with small initialization behaves similarly to the tensor-power method modulo normalization in the first few iterations, bringing the gradient iterates close to the spectral tubal initialization, used, e.g., in
[
27
]
. Here, as before
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
denote the tensor subspace spanned
by the tensor-columns corresponding to the first
r
singular-tubes of tensor
\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
is the tensor-column subspace corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}
. Additionally,
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}_{t}
denotes the tensor-column subspace spanned by the first
r
singular-tubes of the tensor
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}*\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}^{\top}
, where
{\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}^{\top}=\Big(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}\big(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}\big)\Big)^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
. In Figure
2(b)
,
we see that
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
and
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}
learn the subspace
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
almost at the same rate in the first iterations,
1\leq t\lesssim 3000
. In Figure
2(b)
,
we observe that also the angle between
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
, respectively
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}_{t}
, decreases monotonically in the spectral stage. Then at the beginning of the convergence stage,
3000\lesssim t
, the angle between
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
starts decreasing gradually and converges to zero, as expected since
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}
converges to
\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}
. Whereas the principal angle between
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
growths until it reaches a certain plateau.

Figure 4
:
Impact of different initialization scales on the test and the training error. The data are represented in the semi-log plot. We set
n=10,k=4,r=3
in the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
and for initialization
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}=\alpha\,\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}
with
R=200
and different scales of
\alpha
. The plot depicts the averaged value for five runs and the bars represent the deviations from the mean value.

Test and train error under different scales of initialization.

In this experiment, we explore the influence of the initialization scale, denoted by
\alpha
, on the training and the test error. With
R=200
, we apply gradient descent for various values of
\alpha
, halting the iterations at
t=3500
in each run. The results, presented in Figure
4
, demonstrate a reduction in test error as
\alpha
decreases. Notably, the figure indicates that the test error follows an almost polynomial relationship with the initialization scale
\alpha
. This observation is consistent with our theoretical predictions, which also forecast a decrease in test error at a rate of
\alpha
, see Theorem
3.1
.

Impact of different levels of overparameterization on the convergence.

In this numerical analysis, we set
\alpha=10^{-7}
and examined the convergence speed of gradient descent to the ground truth tensor for various overparameterization rates
R
. We run the experiment twenty times for each value of
R
and plot the averaged values per each iteration. The results, shown in Figure
3
, reveal that increasing the number of tensor columns
R
, that is, overparameterizing, accelerates the convergence rate, resulting in fewer iterations to reach the desired error level. Additionally, overparameterization reduces the test error and the training error by affecting the spectral stages.

6
Conclusion and Outlook

In this paper, we focused on studying the implicit regularization of tubal tensor
factorizations via gradient descent by showing that with small random initialization and overparametrization, the gradient descent algorithm is biased towards a low-tubal-rank solution. We have shown that the first iterations of gradient descent with small random initialization behave similarly to the tensor power method, which leads to learning in these first interactions the tensor-column spaces close to the tensor-column space of the ground truth. We also demonstrate that the implicit regularization from small random initialization guides the gradient descent iterations toward low-tubal rank solutions that are not only globally optimal but also generalize well.

Appendix A
Outline of Appendices

In Appendix
B
, we define some additional notation, including the angles between two tensor-column subspaces. In Appendix
C
, we decompose the gradient descent iterates into a “signal” term and a “noise” term, which will aid us in our analysis. In Appendices
D
and
E
, we analyze the spectral and convergence stages, respectively, of the gradient descent iterations. In Appendix
F
, we prove our main result.

To avoid breaking up the flow of our analysis, we put some technical lemmas in the last few appendices instead of in the previously mentioned appendices. In Appendix
G
, we prove some properties of measurement operators which satisfy the restricted isometry property. In Appendix
H
, we prove some properties of matrices and their subspaces. Finally, in Appendix
I
, we prove some properties of random Gaussian tubal tensors.

Appendix B
Additional Notation

For a tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
, we denote its t-SVD by
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}*\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}^{\top}
with the two orthogonal tensor
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}},\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
, and the f-diagonal tensor
\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r\times k}
. We will refer to
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}
as the tensor-column subspace of
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}
and by
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}^{\perp}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(n-r)\times k}
we denote the tensor-column subspace orthogonal to
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}
with its projection operator
{\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}^{\perp}}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}^{\perp}}^{\top}=\mathcal{I}-\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}}^{\top}}
.

We measure the angles between two tensor-column subspaces
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}
and
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
by the tensor-spectral norm
\|\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\|
which according to
[
26
,
11
,
17
]
is equal to

\|\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\|=\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\|=\big\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big\|.

which means that the largest principal angle between
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}
and
\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
equals to that of these two subspaces represented in the Fourier domain. In the Fourier domain, since
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathbb{C}^{(n-r)k\times nk}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathbb{C}^{nk\times nk}
are block diagonal matrices, it holds that

\displaystyle\big\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)}&&&\\
&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)}&&&\\
&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}2}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}\right\|

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\big\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}^{\perp}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big\|

Appendix C
Signal Decomposition

Recall that the gradient descent iterates are defined in (
3.4
) as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\mu\nabla\ell(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}+\mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\left[\bm{y}-\mathcal{A}\left(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}\right)\right]*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu(\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A})\left(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\top}\right)\right]*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}.

For the ground truth tensor
\bm{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
, consider its tensor-column subspace
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
with the corresponding basis
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
. Consider the tensor
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times R\times k}
with its t-SVD decomposition
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{t}*\bm{\Sigma}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}
. For
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times r\times k}
, we denote by
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(n-r)\times k}
a tensor whose tensor-column subspace is orthogonal to those of
\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}
, that is
\|\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}\|=0
and its projection operator
\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}}
is defined as
{\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}}=\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}=\mathcal{I}-\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}}
. We then decompose the gradient descent iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
as follows

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}+\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}

(C.1)

We will refer to the tensors
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}
as the signal term of the gradient descent iterates, and the tensors
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}
will be named as the noise term.

Lemma C.1
.

The tensor-column space of the noise term
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}
is orthogonal to the tensor-column subspace of the
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
, namely
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}=0
.
Moreover, if
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
is full tubal-rank with all invertible singular tubes, then the signal term

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}

has tubal-rank
r
with all invertible singular tubes and the noise term has tubal rank at most
R-r
.

Proof.

\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*(\mathcal{I}-\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top})=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times R\times k}
. The second part follows fact that if
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
is full tubal rank with all invertible singular tubes then all the slices in the Fourier have full rank.
∎

Appendix D
Analysis of the spectral stage

The goal of this section is to show that the first few iterations of the gradient descent algorithm can be approximated by the iteration of the tensor power method modulo normalization defined as

\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}=\Big(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\Big)^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}=\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}.

with the tensor power method iteration
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}=:\Big(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\Big)^{*t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}.

Moreover, this will result in the feature that after the first few iterations, the tensor-column span of the signal term
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}^{\top}
becomes aligned with the tensor-column span of
\bm{\mathcal{X}}
, and that the noise term
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t,\perp}
is relatively small compared to signal term in terms of the norm, indicating that the signal term dominates the noise term.

For this, let us denote the difference between the power method and the gradient descent iterations by

\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}:=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}.

(D.1)

For convenience, throughout this section, we will denote by
\bm{\mathcal{M}}
the tensor
\bm{\mathcal{M}}:=\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
, so that
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}=(\mathcal{I}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
and
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}=(\mathcal{I}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}
.

In the first result of this section, the following lemma, we show that
\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}
can be made small via an appropriate initialization scale.

Lemma D.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}:S^{n\times n\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies
\text{RIP}(2,\delta_{1})
and let
t^{\star}
be defined as

t^{\star}=\min\Big\{j\in\mathbb{N}\colon\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|>\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|\Big\}.

(D.2)

Then for all integers
t
such that
1\leq t\leq t^{\star}
it holds that

\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|=\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\|\leq 8(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\frac{\alpha^{3}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|^{3}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{3t}.

(D.3)

Proof.

Similarly to the matrix case in
[
36
]
, in the tubal tensor case it can be shown that for
t\geq 1
, the difference tensor
\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}
can be represented as

\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*(t-j)}\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}

(D.4)

with
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}=\mu\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}\big(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}^{\top}\big)*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}
.
To estimate
\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|
, we will first estimate each summand in (
D.4
) separately.
First, we can proceed with the following simple estimation

\|(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*(t-j)}\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}\|\leq\|(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})\|^{(t-j)}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}\|\leq\big(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\big)^{(t-j)}\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}\|.

Now, for
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}\|
, using the fact that the spectral norm of tubal tensors is sub-multiplicative, we get that

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{j}\|=\mu\|\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}\big(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}^{\top}\big)*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|\leq\mu\|\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}\big(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}^{\top}\big)\|\cdot\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|.

Since operator
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
\text{RIP}(2,\delta_{1})
, by Lemma
G.3
,
\mathcal{A}
also satisfies
\text{S2NRIP}(\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})
, which provides the following estimate

\|\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}\big(\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}^{\top}\big)\|\leq(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}^{\top}\|_{*}=(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|_{F}^{2}.

All this together leads to

\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|=\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\|\leq\mu(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sum_{j=1}^{t}\big(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\big)^{(t-j)}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|_{F}^{2}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|.

(D.5)

From here, we want to bound
\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|
in terms of the initialization scale
\alpha
and the data-related norm
\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|
. For this, we first use the fact that the tensor Frobenius norm above can be bounded as
\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|
. Then since for all
1\leq j\leq t^{\star}
we have
\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|\leq\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|
, the spectral norm of
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}
can be bounded as

\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}\|\leq\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|+\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{j-1}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|\leq 2\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|.

This gives us the following upper bound

\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|\leq 8\mu(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t-j}\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|^{3}.

(D.6)

As for iterations of the tensor power method, it holds that

\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{j-1}\|=\|(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*(j-1)}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\|\leq\|(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*(j-1)}\|\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\|\leq(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{j-1}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\|=\alpha(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{j-1}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|,

we can proceed with (
D.6
) as follows

\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|\leq 8\mu(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\alpha^{3}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{t}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t+2j-3}.

Now, the sum on the right-hand side can be estimated as

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{t}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t+2j-3}

\displaystyle=(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t-1}\sum_{j=1}^{t}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{2j-2}=(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t-1}\frac{(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{2t}-1}{(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{2}-1}

\displaystyle=(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t-1}\frac{(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{2t}-1}{\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|(2+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)}\leq\frac{(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{3t}}{\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|},

which gives us the final estimation for the norm of
\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}
as follows

\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|\leq 8(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\frac{\alpha^{3}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|^{3}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{3t}

and finishes the proof.
∎

The following lemma provides a lower bound for
t^{\star}
, indicating the duration for which the approximation in Lemma
D.1
remains valid.

Lemma D.2
.

Consider tensors
{\bm{\mathcal{M}}:=\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}}
and
{\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}:=(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
. Let
{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathbb{C}^{nk\times nk}}
be the corresponding block diagonal form of the
tensor
\bm{\mathcal{M}}
with the leading eigenvector
v_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{nk}
, then

t^{\star}\geq\left\lfloor\frac{\ln{\left(\frac{\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\cdot\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}\|_{\ell_{2}}}{8(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\alpha^{3}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|^{3}}\right)}}{2\ln{\left(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\right)}}\right\rfloor

(D.7)

Proof.

Let
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathbb{C}^{nk\times Rk}
be the corresponding block diagonal form of
tensor
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}
. By the definition of the spectral tensor norm, we have
{\|{\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}}\|=\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\|}
and the definition of the matrix norm gives
{\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\|\geq\big\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}\big\|_{\ell_{2}}}
.
For the block diagonal version of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}
, the following properties (see, e.g.,
[
26
]
) holds

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu={\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{t}\cdot\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu.

(D.8)

This allows us to proceed as follows

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}=\big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{t}\cdot\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big)^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{H}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{t}}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}=(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1},

where for the last equality we used the fact that block-diagonal matrix
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
has the same set of eigenvectors as matrix
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. From here, we get
{\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\|\geq\big\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}\big\|_{\ell_{2}}}=(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|)^{t}\big\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}\big\|_{\ell_{2}}
.
Then, applying Lemma
D.1
, the relative error in the spectral norm between
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}
and
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
can be estimated as

\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\|}{\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\|}\leq 8(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\frac{\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\alpha^{3}}{\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\cdot\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}\big\|_{\ell_{2}}}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|^{3}(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\big\|)^{2t}.

Setting the bound above to be smaller than 1 and solving for
t
, we get

t<\frac{\ln{\left(\frac{\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\cdot\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\mathrm{H}}v_{1}\big\|_{\ell_{2}}}{8(1+\delta_{1}\sqrt{k})\sqrt{k\min{\{n,R\}}}\alpha^{3}\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|^{3}}\right)}}{2\ln{\left(1+\mu\|\bm{\mathcal{M}}\|\right)}}.

Since
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
t\leq t^{\star}
should be such that
\frac{\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t-1}-\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t-1}\|}{\|\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t-1}\|}<1
, we can choose
t^{\star}
as the floor-value of the right-hand side above.
∎

To show that the tensor column
subspaces of the tensor power method iterates and the gradient descent iterates are aligned after the alignment phase, we use the largest principal angle between
two tensor-column subspaces as the potential function for analysis. Borrowing
the idea from
[
11
]
, we will show that the power method iteration in the tensor domain can be transformed to the classical subspace iteration in the frequency domain.

For this, consider the power method iterates
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}=(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
, the iterates
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}=(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}
and the gradient descent iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
represented as
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}
. All these tensors have their counterparts in the Fourier domain, which we will denote respectively as
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.

As before, consider
{\bm{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{A}^{*}\mathcal{A}(\bm{\mathcal{X}}*\bm{\mathcal{X}}^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}}
with its t-SVD
\bm{\mathcal{M}}=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}*\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}^{\top}
and its
Fourier domain representative
{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathbb{C}^{nk\times nk}}
. We denote by
\bm{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
the tensor column subspace spanned by the tensor columns corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes, that is
\bm{\mathcal{L}}:=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}(:,1:r,:)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}
.
Note that
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
is also the subspace spanned by the tensor columns corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes of the tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
.

By
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
we will donate the tensor-column subspace
spanned by the tensor columns corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes of the gradient descent iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}
. More concretely, for
{\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\sum_{s=1}^{R}\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}}(:,s,:)*\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}}(s,s,:)*\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}}^{\top}(:,s,:)}
and the corresponding Fourier domain representation
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\operatorname{diag}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)})
, where
{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}=\sum_{\ell}\sigma_{\ell}^{(j)}v_{\ell}^{(j)}{w_{\ell}^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}=U_{U_{t}}^{(j)}\Sigma_{U_{t}}^{(j)}{W_{U_{t}}^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}}
, we define the corresponding new tensors
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}:=\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}}(:,1:r,:)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r\times k}

and their Fourier domain representations

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=\operatorname{diag}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)})

(D.9)

Lemma D.3
.

Consider the tensor iterates
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}=(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}
with its block-matrix representation

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\textrm{bdiag}(\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t})=\textrm{diag}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}).

(D.10)

and the tensors

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{U}}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k}

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\alpha\,\bm{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times R\times k},\quad\alpha>0.

Assume that for each
1\leq j\leq k
, it holds that

\sigma_{r+1}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|+\frac{\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|}{\alpha}<\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu).

(D.11)

Then for each
1\leq j\leq k
, the following two inequalities hold

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{r}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)\geq\alpha\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)-\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|,

(D.12)

\displaystyle\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)\leq\alpha\sigma_{r+1}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|+\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|

(D.13)

Moreover, the principal angle between the tensor-column subspaces
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
is bounded as follows

\|\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}}\|\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\frac{\alpha\sigma_{r+1}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|+\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|}{\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}\big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\top}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu*\bm{\mathcal{U}}}\big)-\alpha\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|-\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|}

(D.14)

Proof.

For some
t\in\mathbb{N}
, consider tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}=(\bm{\mathcal{I}}+\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}
with its block-matrix representation

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\textrm{bdiag}(\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t})=\textrm{diag}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)})=\begin{pmatrix}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)}&&&\\
&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}.

As we assume the symmetric tensor case scenario, the block-diagonal matrix representation
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
consists of symmetric matrices
{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}}
.
At the same time, according to
[
11
]
, the gradient descent tensors

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}
have their block-diagonal matrix representation

\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\;\Leftrightarrow\;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\begin{pmatrix}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)}&&&\\
&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(1)}&&&\\
&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(2)}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}.

(D.15)

Using Weyl’s inequality in each block, we have

\sigma_{r}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)\geq\sigma_{r}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)-\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\|\geq\sigma_{r}\Big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(V_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)-\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\|.

Now, for the singular value above we get the following estimation

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}\Big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(V_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{min}\Big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}V_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{(j)}{V_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{min}\Big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)\sigma_{min}\Big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}\Big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)\geq\alpha\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}\Big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}\Big({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Big)\geq\alpha\sigma_{r}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)})\sigma_{min}\Big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\top}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\Big)

where in the last line we used that for each tensor it holds in the Fourier domain
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu{V_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}}^{(j)}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{H}}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu{\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\mathrm{T}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
.

To show inequality (
D.13
), we can use Weyl’s bounds and then the Courant-Fisher theorem, which leads to

\displaystyle\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)+\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\|\leq\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)+\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\|+\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|\leq\alpha\sigma_{r+1}\big(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\big)\|\bm{\mathcal{U}}\|+\|\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\|.

Now, for estimation of
\|\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{\perp}*\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}}\|
, let us recall that
\bm{\mathcal{L}}
is the tensor column subspace spanned by the tensor columns corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes of tensor
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}=(\bm{\mathcal{I}}-\mu\bm{\mathcal{M}})^{*t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times k}
, and
\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
is the tensor-column subspace spanned by the tensor-columns corresponding to the first
r
singular tubes of the gradient descent iterates
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{t}*\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{0}+\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}
, and consider Fourier-domain representation (
D.15
) of
\bm{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
.
Here, for each
1\leq j\leq k
, the matrices
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
can be represented as

\underbrace{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}_{\widetilde{A}^{(j)}}=\underbrace{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}_{A^{(j)}}+\underbrace{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muE_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}_{C^{(j)}}.

(D.16)

As the tensor-column space
\bm{\mathcal{V}}_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}
is
r
-dimensional, each of matrices
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
has rank
r
, see
[
11
]
.
Since the matrices
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
can be decomposed as

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Sigma_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Sigma_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}

we have that

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\Sigma_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}.

(D.17)

As
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times R}
has rank
r
,
{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
has rank
r
, which means that the product above has rank
r
too. Due to (
D.17
), we see that

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)},

which makes
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
to the column subspace of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muZ_{t}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muU_{0}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}
. Considering the gap between the singular values of for matrices
A^{(j)}
and
\widetilde{A}^{(j)}
in (
D.16
), namely
\delta^{(j)}=\sigma_{r}(A^{(j)})-\sigma_{r+1}(\widetilde{A}^{(j)})
, and using Wedin’s
\sin\theta
theorem
[
41
]
, for each
1\leq j\leq k
we get

\|{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}^{\perp}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}}^{\mathrm{H}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muV_{\bm{\mathcal{L}}_{t}}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(j)}\|\leq\frac{\|C^{(j)}\|}{\delta^{(j)}}.
